\displaystyle\lesssim B\cdot\rho\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_{M}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{1}/\delta)}{(1-\hat{p}^{\mathcal{I}})\cdot N}}+\left(\frac{p^{\mathcal{I}}}{1-p^{\mathcal{I}}}+\frac{\hat{p}^{\mathcal{I}}}{1-\hat{p}^{\mathcal{I}}}\right)\cdot B^{2}

Combining
(a)
and
(b)
using the union bound, we have with probability at least
1-2M\cdot\delta
:

\displaystyle\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho,p)}\left[{\Vhc_{{\color[rgb]{0.1992,0.5586,0.6563}+},\mathcal{E}}[{\ell_{{\color[rgb]{0.457,0.6172,0.2227}\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{E}}}]-\Vhc_{{\color[rgb]{0.1992,0.5586,0.6563}+},\mathcal{E}}[{\hat{\ell}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1992,0.5586,0.6563}\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{E}}}]}\right]\lesssim B\cdot\rho\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_{M}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{1}/\delta)}{(1-\hat{p}^{\mathcal{I}})\cdot N}}+\left(\frac{p^{\mathcal{I}}}{1-p^{\mathcal{I}}}+\frac{\hat{p}^{\mathcal{I}}}{1-\hat{p}^{\mathcal{I}}}\right)\cdot B^{2}

(26)

Finally, by combining Eq.(
23
) and Eq.(
26
), we conclude that with probability at least
1-(2M+1)\cdot\delta
:

\displaystyle\Ec_{\mathcal{E}}[{\ell_{{\color[rgb]{0.457,0.6172,0.2227}\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{E}}}]\lesssim\Ehc_{\mathcal{E}}[{\ell_{{\color[rgb]{0.457,0.6172,0.2227}\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{E}}}]+\sqrt{\frac{\Vhc_{{\color[rgb]{0.1992,0.5586,0.6563}+},\mathcal{E}}[{\hat{\ell}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1992,0.5586,0.6563}\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{E}}}]\cdot\log\left(C_{M}\cdot\mathcal{M}/\delta\right)}{(1-\hat{p}^{\mathcal{M}})^{2}\cdot M}}+\frac{\rho\cdot\log\left(C_{M}\cdot\mathcal{M}/\delta\right)}{(1-\hat{p}^{\mathcal{M}})\cdot M}+\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}+\Delta^{\mathcal{M}}.

Here, we ignore lower-order terms involving both
N^{-1/2}
and
M^{-1/2}
.

Final Step.
By combining the bounds for (1), (2), and (3), we obtain the following result, which holds with probability at least
1-\delta
uniformly for all
f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho,p)
:

\displaystyle\Ec_{\mathcal{E}}[{\ell_{{\color[rgb]{0.457,0.6172,0.2227}\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{E}}}]\lesssim

\displaystyle~\Ehc_{\mathcal{E}}[{\hat{\ell}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1992,0.5586,0.6563}\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{E}}}]+\frac{B\cdot\|\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}{C!}+\sqrt{\frac{\Vhc_{{\color[rgb]{0.1992,0.5586,0.6563}+},\mathcal{E}}[{\hat{\ell}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1992,0.5586,0.6563}\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{E}}}]\cdot\log\left(C_{M}\cdot\mathcal{M}/\delta\right)}{(1-\hat{p}^{\mathcal{M}})^{2}\cdot M}}+\frac{\rho^{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\log\left(C_{M}\cdot\mathcal{M}/\delta\right)}{(1-\hat{p}^{\mathcal{M}})\cdot M}

\displaystyle+\rho^{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(C_{M}\cdot\mathcal{N}_{1}/\delta)}{(1-\hat{p}^{\mathcal{I}})\cdot N}}+\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}+\Delta^{\mathcal{M}},

This result follows from the assumption that

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\infty}(\mathcal{F},{\epsilon},{M})\leq\left(\frac{r}{\epsilon}\right)^{\nu}.

∎

Corollary 2

(
Tigher Bound with Light-tail Loss Decay
)
.

Based on Thm.3 in our paper, suppose that the loess satisfies an asymptotic exponential-tail condition: there exists
\alpha_{0}>0
, such that for all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}:=\min(N^{-1/\alpha_{0}},M^{-1/\alpha_{0}})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{E}}[\ell(f(x),y,P_{i})]\geq\tau\right]\lesssim\exp(-\lambda_{1}\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{E}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}}[\ell(f(x),y,P_{i})]\geq\tau\right]\lesssim\exp(-\lambda_{2}\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{P}}_{x}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{E}}[\ell(f(x),y,P_{i})]\geq\tau\right]\lesssim\exp(-\hat{\lambda}_{1}\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{E}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}}[\ell(f(x),y,P_{i})]\geq\tau\right]\lesssim\exp(-\hat{\lambda}_{2}\cdot\tau)

and
\lambda_{1},\hat{\lambda}_{1}\asymp\log(N)\cdot N^{1/\alpha_{0}},\lambda_{2},\hat{\lambda}_{2}\asymp\log(M)\cdot M^{1/\alpha_{0}}
, then we can pick:

\displaystyle\rho^{\mathcal{M}}\asymp M^{-1/\alpha_{0}},\rho^{\mathcal{I}}\asymp N^{-1/\alpha_{0}},p^{\mathcal{I}}\asymp\frac{1}{N},p^{\mathcal{M}}\asymp\frac{1}{M}

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}^{\mathcal{M}}\asymp M^{-1/\alpha_{0}},\hat{\rho}^{\mathcal{I}}\asymp N^{-1/\alpha_{0}},\hat{p}^{\mathcal{I}}\asymp\frac{1}{N},\hat{p}^{\mathcal{M}}\asymp\frac{1}{M}

such that
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\lesssim\frac{1}{N},\Delta^{\mathcal{M}}\lesssim\frac{1}{M}
, and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Err}_{sto}=

\displaystyle\frac{\nu\cdot\log\left(\zeta_{1}\right)}{(1-\hat{p}^{\mathcal{M}})\cdot{M^{{1+1/\alpha_{0}}}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\nu\cdot\log(\zeta_{2})}{(1-\hat{p}^{\mathcal{I}})\cdot{N^{{1+2/\alpha_{0}}}}}}

Proof.

For the sake of simplicity, we only prove the case for
\rho^{\mathcal{I}}
and
p^{\mathcal{I}}
, the remaining cases follow similarly.

Based on the assumption, for all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}[\ell(f(x),y,p_{i})\geq\tau]

\displaystyle\lesssim\exp(-\lambda_{1}\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle\asymp\exp(-\log(N)\cdot N^{1/\alpha_{0}}\cdot\tau)

Then, by selecting
\rho^{\mathcal{I}}\asymp N^{-1/\alpha}
and substituting
\tau=\rho^{\mathcal{I}}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}[\ell\geq\rho^{\mathcal{I}}]\lesssim\exp(-\log(N)\cdot N^{-1/\alpha_{0}}\cdot N^{-1/\alpha})=\frac{1}{N}

Thus,

\displaystyle p^{\mathcal{I}}\asymp\frac{1}{N}

which verifies that the predefined
\rho^{\mathcal{I}}
and
p^{\mathcal{I}}
are compatible under the assumed tail behavior. The same reasoning applies to the remaining quantities.
∎

Appendix G
Proof of the PEFT Framework

G-A

Definitions and Preliminaries

Definition 4

(Stiefel Manifold)
.

The Stiefel manifold
St(N+K,K)
is the set of all ordered orthonormal
K
-tuples in
\mathbb{R}^{N+K}
:

St(N+K,K):=\left\{\bm{V}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{K})\in\mathbb{R}^{(N+K)\times K}\mid\bm{V}^{\top}\bm{V}=\bm{I}_{K}\right\}.

Definition 5

(Special Orthogonal Group Action)
.

The special orthogonal group
\mathrm{SO}(N)
is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{SO}(N)=\left\{\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}\mid\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\top}=\bm{X}^{\top}\bm{X}=\bm{I}_{N},~\det(\bm{X})=1\right\}

G-B

Result on the Tight Covering Number

Lemma 9
.

Given the restricted Stiefel manifold
St(n,k)
, the covering number satisfies the following upper bound:

\displaystyle\log\left(\mathcal{N}(St(n,k),||\cdot||_{op},\epsilon)\right)\lesssim\left(nk-\frac{(k+1)k}{2}\right)\cdot\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)

Proof.

The proof follows from verifying the conditions of Thm. 8 in
[
50
]
.
By setting
N+K=n
and
K=k
, Lem.
12
implies that
St(n,k)\cong SO(n)/SO(n-k)
. Moreover, by Lem.
13
, we have
||\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{x}}||=1
. Applying Thm. 8 in
[
50
]
then completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 10
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(r,m,n)
denote the set of all
m\times n
real matrices
\bm{A}
with rank at most
r
and Frobenius norm bounded by
R
, i.e.,
||\bm{A}||_{F}\leq R
. Then its metric entropy satisfies the following upper bound:

\displaystyle\log\left(\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M}(r,m,n),\epsilon,||\cdot||_{F})\right)\leq\left(r(m+n)-r^{2}\right)\cdot\log\left(\frac{(2R+1)^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)

Proof.

We aim to derive an
\epsilon
-covering number
N(\epsilon,\mathcal{M}(r,m,n),\|\cdot\|)
under the Frobenius norm
\|\cdot\|_{F}
.

a) Singular Decomposition.
Any matrix
A\in\mathcal{M}(r,m,n)
can be decomposed using singular value decomposition (SVD):

A=U\Sigma V^{\top}

where:

•

U\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times r}
has orthonormal columns,

•

V\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
has orthonormal columns,

•

\Sigma=\text{diag}(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{r})
with
\sigma_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{r}\geq 0
.

b) Parameterization and Constraints.

We consider the following classes for
U,\Sigma,V
:

1.

The diagonal matrix
\Sigma
belongs to the class:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}:=\left\{\Sigma=\text{diag}(\sigma)\mid\sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{r},\|\sigma\|_{F}\leq R\right\}.

2.

The matrices
U,V
belong to the Stiefel manifolds:
U\in\text{St}(m,r),V\in\text{St}(n,r)
.

c) Covering Construction.

We will construct separate covers for each component:

1.

For the singular values
\Sigma
, we use a grid with radius
\epsilon/(2R+1)
. This set can be covered with
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M}_{1},||\cdot||_{\infty},\epsilon/(2R+1))
open balls in the
\ell_{\infty}
-norm. We have the bound
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M}_{1},||\cdot||_{\infty},\epsilon)\leq r\cdot\log(R/\epsilon)
.

2.

For
U\in\text{St}(m,r)
, with a radius of
\epsilon/(2R+1)
, we can cover this set with
\mathcal{N}(\text{St}(m,r),||\cdot||_{op},\epsilon)
open balls. The bound is
\log\left(\mathcal{N}(\text{St}(m,r),||\cdot||_{op},\epsilon)\right)\leq(mr-(r+1)r/2)\cdot\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)
(Lem.
9
).

3.

For
V\in\text{St}(n,r)
, with a radius of
\epsilon/(2R+1)
, we can cover this set with
\mathcal{N}(\text{St}(n,r),||\cdot||_{op},\epsilon)
open balls. The bound is
\log\left(\mathcal{N}(\text{St}(n,r),||\cdot||_{op},\epsilon)\right)\leq(nr-(r+1)r/2)\cdot\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)
(Lem.
9
).

d) Error Decomposition.
We now use an error decomposition scheme to reduce the covering number counting problem for
\mathcal{M}(r,m,n)
to three simpler classes.
For any matrix
A=U\Sigma V^{\top}
, let
\hat{A}=\hat{U}\hat{\Sigma}\hat{V}^{\top}
be its closest approximation in the three series of open balls constructed above. The error decomposes as:

\displaystyle\|A-\hat{A}\|_{F}

\displaystyle=\|U\Sigma V^{\top}-\hat{U}\hat{\Sigma}\hat{V}^{\top}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\|U\Sigma V^{\top}-\hat{U}\Sigma V^{\top}\|_{F}+\|\hat{U}\Sigma V^{\top}-\hat{U}\hat{\Sigma}V^{\top}\|_{F}+\|\hat{U}\hat{\Sigma}V^{\top}-\hat{U}\hat{\Sigma}\hat{V}^{\top}\|_{F}

\displaystyle=\|(U-\hat{U})\Sigma V^{\top}\|_{F}+\|\hat{U}(\Sigma-\hat{\Sigma})V^{\top}\|_{F}+\|\hat{U}\hat{\Sigma}(V-\hat{V})^{\top}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\|U-\hat{U}\|_{op}\|\Sigma\|_{F}+\|\Sigma-\hat{\Sigma}\|_{op}+\|\hat{\Sigma}\|_{F}\|V-\hat{V}\|_{op}

\displaystyle=\|U-\hat{U}\|_{op}\|A\|_{F}+\|\Sigma-\hat{\Sigma}\|_{op}+\|\hat{A}\|_{F}\|V-\hat{V}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2R+1}\cdot R+\frac{\epsilon}{2R+1}+R\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{2R+1}=\epsilon

Using the basic property of the covering number, the total
\epsilon
-covering number becomes the product of individual
\epsilon/(2R+1)
-covers of the three classes. Taking the logarithm and using the results from part (c), we obtain:

\displaystyle\log\left(\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M}(r,m,n),||\cdot||_{F},\epsilon)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\log(\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M}_{1},||\cdot||_{\infty},\tilde{\epsilon}))+\log\left(\mathcal{N}(\text{St}(m,r),||\cdot||_{op},\tilde{\epsilon})\right)+\log\left(\mathcal{N}(\text{St}(n,r),||\cdot||_{op},\tilde{\epsilon})\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(r(m+n)-r^{2}\right)\cdot\log\left(\frac{(2R+1)^{2}}{\epsilon}\right),

where
\tilde{\epsilon}=\epsilon/(2R+1)
.
∎

G-C

Proof of the Main Result

We define the following hypothesis classes, which are consistent across all tasks:

1.

The parameter class of the
l
-th layer of the transformer in LoRA-LSF that involves parameter updates:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{LoRA}}_{l}=\left\{\Delta\bm{\theta}_{l}=\bm{A}_{l}\bm{B}_{l}^{\top}\mid\bm{A}=\sum_{j}w_{A,j,l}\cdot\bm{A}_{(j),l},\quad\bm{B}=\sum_{j}w_{B,j,l}\cdot\bm{B}_{(j),l},\|\Delta\bm{\theta}_{l}\|_{F}\leq R\right\}

where
*_{l}
denotes the corresponding parameter for the
l
-th layer.

2.

The overall parameter class:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{LoRA}}=\bigotimes_{l=1}^{n_{L}}{\mathsf{LoRA}}_{l}

3.

The parameter class of the
l
-th layer of the transformer in Adapter-LSF that involves parameter updates:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adapter}_{l}=\left\{\Delta\bm{\theta}_{l}=\left\{\bm{W}_{\downarrow,l},\bm{W}_{\uparrow,l}\right\}\mid\bm{W}_{\downarrow,l}=\sum_{j}w_{\downarrow,j,l}\cdot\bm{W}_{\downarrow,(j),l},\quad\bm{W}_{\uparrow,l}=\sum_{j}w_{\uparrow,j,l}\cdot\bm{W}_{\uparrow,(j),l},\|\Delta\bm{\theta}_{l}\|_{F}\leq R\right\}

4.

The overall parameter class:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Adapter}}=\bigotimes_{l=1}^{n_{L}}{\mathsf{Adapter}}_{l}

5.

The hypothesis class for the score function for LoRA-LSF:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{LoRA}}=\left\{f^{(i)}_{\bm{\Theta}^{i}}(\cdot),i=1,2,\cdots,N_{K}\mid f^{(i)}\text{ are linearized scores as in Eq.\ref{eq:lin}},\text{ the overall parameter set }\Delta{\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}}\in{\mathsf{LoRA}},\forall i\right\}

6.

The hypothesis class for the score function for Adapter-LSF:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{Adapter}}=\left\{f^{(i)}_{\bm{\Theta}^{i}}(\cdot),i=1,2,\cdots,N_{K}\mid f^{(i)}\text{ are linearized scores as in Eq.\ref{eq:lin}},\text{ the overall parameter set }\Delta{\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}}\in{\mathsf{Adapter}},\forall i\right\}

Claim 1
.

Given the linearized hypothesis class
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{LoRA}}
, if the parameter class is
\epsilon/L_{f}
-compact, then its covering number satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{LoRA}},\epsilon,\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq\mathcal{N}({\mathsf{LoRA}},\epsilon/L_{f},\|\cdot\|_{2})^{n_{K}}.

Proof.

For any
i\in[n_{K}]
, and for any pair of functions
f,\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{LoRA}}
, such that the parameter set is
\epsilon/L_{f}
-compact, we have the following inequality based on Eq. (
35
) and Assumption 1:

\displaystyle|f^{(i)}(\bm{x})-\tilde{f}^{(i)}(\bm{x})|\leq L_{f}\cdot\|\Delta{\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}}-\Delta{\widetilde{\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}}}\|\leq L_{f}\cdot\max_{i=1,\cdots,n_{K}}\|\Delta{\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}}-\Delta{\widetilde{\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}}}\|\bm{:=}L_{f}\cdot\|\Delta{\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}}-\Delta{\widetilde{\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}}}\|_{2,\infty}

(27)

Based on the definition of
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{LoRA}}
, we know that the incremental parameters must come from
\mathsf{LoRA}^{n_{K}}
, since for each expert we need to choose a parameter from
\mathsf{LoRA}
, leading to a product space. Since the parameter set is
\epsilon/L_{f}
-compact, the arguments in (
27
) hold. In this case, choosing a radius of
\epsilon/L_{f}
is valid. From (
27
), we know that a
\epsilon/L_{f}
-cover of
\mathsf{LoRA}^{n_{K}}
must induce a
\epsilon
-cover of
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{LoRA}}
, which implies:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{LoRA}},\epsilon,\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq\mathcal{N}({\mathsf{LoRA}}^{n_{K}},\epsilon/L_{f},\|\cdot\|_{2,\infty})

Next, we bound the result using the covering number of a single copy of the incremental parameter from
\mathsf{LoRA}
. Specifically, let
\mathcal{K}=\left\{\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{\mathcal{N}}\right\}
be a covering of
\mathsf{LoRA}
with radius
\epsilon/L_{f}
. For an element
\Delta{\bm{\Theta}}=\left(\Delta{\bm{\Theta}^{(1)}},\cdots,\Delta{\bm{\Theta}^{(n_{K})}}\right)\in\mathsf{LoRA}^{n_{K}}
, we must cover each
\Delta{\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}}
with elements from
\mathcal{K}
(taking the maximum of the norm requires covering the incremental parameters for each task). In this sense,
\mathcal{K}^{n_{K}}
induces a cover of
\mathsf{LoRA}^{n_{K}}
with the desired norm. Therefore, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}({\mathsf{LoRA}}^{n_{K}},\epsilon/L_{f},\|\cdot\|_{2,\infty})\leq\mathcal{N}({\mathsf{LoRA}},\epsilon/L_{f},\|\cdot\|_{2})^{n_{K}}

∎

Claim 2
.

Given the linearized hypothesis class
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{LoRA}}
, if the parameter class is
\epsilon/L_{f}
-compact, then its covering number satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{LoRA}},\epsilon,\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq n_{L}\cdot(Kr\cdot(m+n)-K^{2}r^{2})\cdot\log\left(L_{f}\cdot n_{L}\cdot R/\epsilon\right)

Proof.

According to Claim
1
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{LoRA}},\epsilon,\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq\mathcal{N}({\mathsf{LoRA}},\epsilon/L_{f},\|\cdot\|_{2})^{n_{K}}.

By definition, we have:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{LoRA}}=\bigotimes_{l=1}^{n_{L}}{\mathsf{LoRA}}_{l}\subseteq\mathcal{M}(Kr,m,n)^{n_{L}}

Using the property of the covering number for a product space and Lem.
11
, we get:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{LoRA}},\epsilon,\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq\prod_{l=1}^{n_{L}}\left(\mathcal{N}({\mathsf{LoRA}}_{l},\|\cdot\|_{F},\epsilon/(L_{f}\cdot n_{L}))\right)^{n_{K}}\leq\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M}(Kr,m,n),\epsilon/(L_{f}\cdot n_{L}),\|\cdot\|_{F})^{n_{L}\cdot n_{K}}

Combining all results, we obtain:

\displaystyle\log\left(\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{LoRA}},\epsilon,\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\right)\leq n_{L}\cdot n_{K}\cdot(Kr\cdot(m+n)-K^{2}r^{2})\cdot\log\left(L_{f}\cdot n_{L}\cdot R/\epsilon\right)

where the last inequality follows from Lem.
10
.
∎

Claim 3
.

Given the linearized hypothesis class
\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{Adapter}}
, where
f
is the linearized logit, its covering number satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{F}^{\mathsf{Adapter}},\epsilon,\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq 2\cdot n_{L}\cdot(r\cdot(m+n)-r^{2})\cdot\log\left(L_{f}\cdot n_{L}\cdot R/\epsilon\right)

Proof.

The proof follows similarly to the previous claim and is omitted.
∎

Proof of the result in Thm.
4
.

Since the loss function is 1-Lipschitz, for a score function
f^{(1)},\cdots,f^{(n_{K})}
and the corresponding loss
\ell
, we denote its linearized approximation in Eq.
35
as
f^{\prime(1)},\cdots,f^{\prime(n_{K})}
with the corresponding loss
\ell^{\prime}
. Using the residue
\epsilon
from the Taylor expansion in Eq.
35
, we have:

\displaystyle\Ec_{\mathcal{E}}[{\ell_{{\color[rgb]{0.457,0.6172,0.2227}\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{E}}}]\leq\Ec_{\mathcal{E}}[{\ell_{{\color[rgb]{0.457,0.6172,0.2227}\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}}\left[\max_{\theta\in\mathsf{LinSeg}(\Delta\theta^{(i)},\Delta\theta^{(i),\star})}\left\|\bm{H}_{\theta}(\bm{x})\right\|_{op}\cdot\|\Delta\theta^{(i)}-\Delta\theta^{(i),\star}\|_{F}^{2}\right]

(28)

Similarly, we apply the same principle to the empirical loss terms, leading to the following inequality:

\displaystyle\Ehc_{\mathcal{E}}[{\hat{\ell}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1992,0.5586,0.6563}\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}]\leq\Ehc_{\mathcal{E}}[{\hat{\ell}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1992,0.5586,0.6563}\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{E}}}]+\hat{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{D}}\left[\max_{\theta\in\mathsf{LinSeg}(\Delta\theta^{(i)},\Delta\theta^{(i),\star})}\left\|\bm{H}_{\theta}(\bm{x})\right\|_{op}\cdot\|\Delta\theta^{(i)}-\Delta\theta^{(i),\star}\|_{F}^{2}\right]

(29)

The proof then follows by applying Thm.
3
and Clms.
2
and
3
to bound the gap
\Ec_{\mathcal{E}}[{\ell_{{\color[rgb]{0.457,0.6172,0.2227}\mathcal{D}},\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}]-\Ehc_{\mathcal{E}}[{\hat{\ell}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1992,0.5586,0.6563}\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{E}}}^{\prime}]
, and using Eq.
28
-
29
to bound the residual error of the linear approximation.
∎

G-D

Additional Tools for the Proof

Lemma 11
.

For LoRA-LSF, the hypothesis class satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathsf{LoRA}^{(i)}_{l}\subseteq\mathcal{M}(Kr,m,n)

For Adapter-LSF, the hypothesis class satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adapter}^{(i)}_{l}\subseteq\mathcal{M}(r,m,n)\otimes\mathcal{M}(r,m,n)

Proof.

dsaad

LoRA-LSF.
Since
\bm{B}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
, we have
\mathrm{rank}(\bm{B}^{(i)})\leq r
. Therefore:

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\left(w^{i}_{A,j,l}\cdot\bm{A}_{(j),l}\cdot\bm{B}^{(i),l}\right)\leq r.

Using the fact that
\mathrm{rank}(A+B)\leq\mathrm{rank}(A)+\mathrm{rank}(B)
, we get:

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\left(\bm{A}^{(i)}_{l}\bm{B}^{(i)^{\top}}_{l}\right)\leq Kr.

Thus,
\bm{A}^{(i)}_{l}\bm{B}^{(i)^{\top}}_{l}\in\mathcal{M}(Kr,m,n)
.

Adapter-LSF.
The proof follows similarly by bounding the rank of
\bm{W}^{(i)}_{\downarrow,l}
and
\bm{W}^{(i)}_{\uparrow,l}
, and is thus omitted.

∎

Geometrical Properties.
We now show a fundamental property of the Stiefel manifold.

Lemma 12
.

St(N+K,K)\cong\mathrm{SO}(N+K)/\mathrm{SO}(N)
. In other words, the Stiefel manifold
St(N+K,K)
is diffeomorphic to the homogeneous space
\mathrm{SO}(N+K)/\mathrm{SO}(N)
.

G-D
1
Proof

Claim 4

(
Transitivity of the Group Action
)
.

The
\mathrm{SO}(N+K)
-action on
St(N+K,K)
is transitive.

Proof.

For any two
K
-frames
\bm{V}=(v_{i})_{i=1}^{K}
and
\bm{W}=(w_{i})_{i=1}^{K}\in St(N+K,K)
, extend them to full orthonormal bases of
\mathbb{R}^{N+K}
, resulting in
\bm{\tilde{V}},\bm{\tilde{W}}
, such that:

\displaystyle\bm{\tilde{V}}=(\bm{V};v_{K+1},\dots,v_{N+K})\in O(N+K),\quad\bm{\tilde{W}}=(\bm{W};w_{K+1},\dots,w_{N+K})\in O(N+K).

Then, there exists a basis transfer mapping
\bm{A}
such that:

\displaystyle\bm{A}\bm{\tilde{V}}=\bm{\tilde{W}}.

It follows that:

\displaystyle\bm{A}=\bm{\tilde{W}}\bm{\tilde{V}}^{-1}.

Since
\bm{A}\in O(N+K)
, we now need to show that for every such
\bm{A}
, there exists a
\bm{A}^{\prime}\in SO(N+K)
that also realizes the basis transfer between the original
K
-frames. Specifically, construct a diagonal matrix:

\displaystyle\bm{R}=\text{diag}(1,\dots,-1^{\det(\bm{A})}).

We can then construct a mapping
\bm{A}^{\prime}\in SO(N+K)
such that:

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\prime}=\bm{\tilde{W}}\bm{R}\bm{\tilde{V}}^{-1}.

Thus, we have:

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\prime}\bm{\tilde{V}}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}^{\prime}(\bm{V};\bm{v}_{K+1},\dots,\bm{v}_{N+K})

\displaystyle=\bm{\tilde{W}}\bm{R}=(\bm{W};\bm{w}_{K+1},\dots,(-1)^{\det(\bm{A})}\bm{w}_{N+K}).

Performing blockwise calculations, we confirm:

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\prime}\bm{V}=\bm{W}.

Since the choice of
\bm{V}
and
\bm{W}
is arbitrary, we conclude that for all
\bm{V},\bm{W}\in St(N+K,K)
, there exists
\bm{A}^{\prime}\in SO(N+K)
such that
\bm{A}^{\prime}\bm{V}=\bm{W}
. Thus, we have proved the claim that for all
\bm{V}\in St(N+K,K)
,
\text{Orbit}(\bm{V})=St(N+K,K)
.
∎

Claim 5
.

For the
K
-frame
\bm{E}_{K}=(e_{1},\dots,e_{K})
, the stabilizer subgroup, defined as:

\mathrm{Stab}(\bm{E}_{K})=\left\{\bm{B}\in\mathrm{SO}(N+K)\mid\bm{B}\bm{E}_{K}=\bm{E}_{K},\ 1\leq i\leq K\right\},

satisfies:

\mathrm{Stab}(\bm{E}_{K})\cong\mathrm{SO}(N).

Proof.

First, we rewrite the mapping
\bm{B}\in\mathrm{SO}(N+K)
in block form:

\displaystyle\bm{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{B}_{1}&\bm{B}_{2}\\
\bm{B}_{3}&\bm{B}_{4}\end{pmatrix}.

Since
\bm{B}\bm{E}_{K}=\bm{E}_{K}
, we have:

\displaystyle\bm{B}_{1}=\bm{I}_{K},\quad\bm{B}_{3}=0.

Moreover, since
\bm{B}^{\top}\bm{B}=\bm{B}\bm{B}^{\top}=\bm{I}_{N+K}
, we also have:

\displaystyle\bm{B}_{2}^{\top}\bm{B}_{2}=0,\quad\bm{B}_{4}^{\top}\bm{B}_{4}=\bm{B}_{4}\bm{B}_{4}^{\top}=\bm{I}_{N}.

Thus,
\bm{B}
must satisfy:

\displaystyle\bm{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{I}_{K}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{B}_{4}\end{pmatrix},

and therefore:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Stab}(\bm{E}_{K})=\left\{\bm{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{I}_{K}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{B}_{4}\end{pmatrix},\,\bm{B}_{4}\in SO(N)\right\}.

It is straightforward to verify that the mapping:

\displaystyle\phi:\begin{pmatrix}\bm{I}_{K}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{B}_{4}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\bm{B}_{4}

is a smooth group homomorphism. Thus, the claim is proven by definition.
∎

Claim 6
.

The Stiefel manifold
St(N+K,K)
is diffeomorphic to the homogeneous space
\mathrm{SO}(N+K)/\mathrm{SO}(N)
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the Orbit-Stabilizer Theorem of the Lie Group (see Thm. 4.4.3 in
[
60
]
and Chap. 2 in
[
61
]
):

\displaystyle St(N+K,K)=\mathrm{Orbit}(\bm{E}_{K})\cong\mathrm{SO}(N+K)/\mathrm{Stab}(\bm{E}_{K})\cong\mathrm{SO}(N+K)/\mathrm{SO}(N).

∎

Bound of the Operator Norm.
In this proof, we use the Lie algebra of
SO(N)
, which is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{so}(N)=\left\{\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}\mid\bm{X}+\bm{X}^{\top}=\bm{0}\right\}.

In other words, it is the subspace of skew-symmetric matrices.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{so}(N+K)
be the Lie algebra of
SO(N+K)
,
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{so}(N)
be the Lie algebra of
SO(N)
, and
\mathfrak{x}
be the orthogonal complement of
\mathfrak{h}
in
\mathfrak{g}
. Now, we derive the operator norm of the projection onto
\mathfrak{x}
with respect to the Frobenius norm, denoted as
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{x}}
.

Lemma 13
.

Given the definitions above, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{x}}=1.

Proof.

First, for any
\bm{X}\in\mathfrak{h}
, we can embed it in
\mathfrak{g}
as:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\bm{X}&\bm{0}\\
\bm{0}&\bm{0}\end{pmatrix}

This is an element of
\mathfrak{g}
. It is easy to see that the complement,
\mathfrak{x}
, consists of matrices of the form:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{x}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\bm{0}&\bm{B}\\
-\bm{B}^{\top}&\bm{D}\end{pmatrix},\bm{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times K},\bm{D}\in\mathfrak{so}(K)\right\}

Furthermore, any
\bm{A}\in\mathfrak{g}
must have the form:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\bm{A}&\bm{B}\\
-\bm{B}^{\top}&\bm{D}\end{pmatrix},\bm{A}\in\mathfrak{so}(N),\bm{D}\in\mathfrak{so}(K),\bm{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{(N-K)\times K}\right\}

This shows that if
\bm{X}\in\mathfrak{g}
has the form:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\bm{A}&\bm{B}\\
-\bm{B}^{\top}&\bm{D}\end{pmatrix},

then

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{x}}(\bm{X})=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{0}&\bm{B}\\
-\bm{B}^{\top}&\bm{D}\end{pmatrix}

Since the operator
\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{x}}(\bm{X})
is linear and satisfies
\mathcal{P}^{2}_{\mathfrak{x}}(\bm{X})=\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{x}}(\bm{X})
, we immediately have
\|\mathcal{P}_{\mathfrak{x}}(\bm{X})\|=1
.

∎

Appendix H
Validity of the Covering Number Assumption

The covering number assumption

\mathcal{N}_{\infty}(\mathcal{F},\varepsilon,M)\lesssim\Bigl(\frac{r}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{\nu},\qquad 0<\varepsilon\leq r,

(30)

is not restrictive—it generally holds for bounded high-dimensional spaces under the
\ell_{\infty}
metric
.

Below, we explain why this is the case. First, we show that this assumption
originates from a general result on covering a high-dimensional
\ell_{\infty}
ball
, which provides a universal upper bound of the above form. Then, we demonstrate that this result indeed holds for
a wide range of common model families
, including single-layer linear models, fully connected DNNs, CNNs, and LoRA/LoRA-LSF. Hence,
the covering number assumption is theoretically grounded and broadly valid in practical scenarios
.

H-A

General High-Dimensional Ball Covering

Lemma 14

(grid cover of an
\ell_{\infty}
-ball
[
41
]
)
.

Let
B^{\nu}_{\infty}(r)=\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}:\|\theta\|_{\infty}\leq r\}
be a ball of radius
r
in
\nu
dimensions. For any
0<\varepsilon\leq r
, the covering number satisfies:

\mathcal{N}\bigl(B^{\nu}_{\infty}(r),\varepsilon;\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim\bigl(\frac{3r}{\varepsilon}\bigr)^{\nu}.

Lemma 15

(Lipschitz push-forward
[
41
]
)
.

Let
(\Theta,\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
be a parameter space,
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{\theta}:\theta\in\Theta\}
a function class on
\mathcal{X}
, and assume

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\|f_{\theta}(x)-f_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)\|_{\infty}\leq L\,\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\quad\text{for all }\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta.

Then, we have:

\mathcal{N}_{\infty}(\mathcal{F},\varepsilon,M)\leq\mathcal{N}(\Theta,\varepsilon/L;\|\cdot\|_{\infty})

Corollary 3
.

If
\Theta\subseteq B_{\infty}^{\nu}(R)
and the mapping
\theta\mapsto f_{\theta}
is
L
-Lipschitz as above, then by Lemma.
14
and Lemma.
15
,

\mathcal{N}_{\infty}(\mathcal{F},\varepsilon,M)\leq\Bigl(\frac{3RL}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{\nu}\lesssim\Bigl(\frac{r}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{\nu},

where
r:=3RL
.

Thus, the assumption directly follows from covering an
\ell_{\infty}
ball and a Lipschitz parameter-to-function map. Next, we show that this Lipschitz condition holds for
many common model families
, so the covering number assumption applies to them. Therefore, the covering number assumption is reasonable and broadly valid across practical cases.

H-B

Applications

Throughout, we assume the input set
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\{x:\|x\|_{\infty}\leq M\}
. For any matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, define:

•

\|W\|_{\max}=\max_{i,j}\|W_{ij}\|
(entrywise
\ell_{\infty}
),

•

\|W\|_{\infty}=\max_{i}\sum_{j}\|W_{ij}\|
(operator norm for
\ell_{\infty}\to\ell_{\infty}
)

Note that
\|W\|_{\max}\leq\|W\|_{\infty}
and
\|W\|_{\infty}\leq n\|W\|_{\max}
.

H-B
1
Single-Layer Linear Models

Let
f_{\theta}(x)=Wx+b
be a single-layer linear model with
W\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The parameter set is denoted as
\Theta=\{\theta=(W,b)\ \big|\ \|\theta\|_{\infty}:=\max(\|W\|_{\max},\|b\|_{\infty})\leq R\}
with
\nu=mn+n
dimensions.

For any
\theta=(W,b),\,\theta^{\prime}=(W^{\prime},b^{\prime})
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we have:

\displaystyle\|f_{\theta}(x)-f_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|(W-W^{\prime})x+(b-b^{\prime})\|_{\infty}

(31)

\displaystyle\leq\|W-W^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\|x\|_{\infty}+\|b-b^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 2n\|W-W^{\prime}\|_{\max}\,M+\|b-b^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq(2nM+1)\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

Thus, the mapping
\theta\mapsto f_{\theta}
is
L=(2nM+1)
-Lipschitz under the parameter
\ell_{\infty}
norm.
By Lemma.
14
and Lemma.
15
,

\mathcal{N}_{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{\text{linear}},\varepsilon,M)\leq\Bigl(\frac{3R(2nM+1)}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{mn+n}\lesssim\Bigl(\frac{r}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{\nu}\quad(\nu=mn+n,\ r=6RnM+3R).

H-B
2
DNNs

We consider DNNs with complicated conv layers. Specifically, in the following proposition, we consider a set of DNNs with sparsity constraints in each layer. Note that we introduce sparsity since most real-world NNs can be regarded as a specific kind of sparse DNN. Moreover, the result also covers fully connected DNNs by setting
s_{l}=d_{l-1}d_{l}
.

Proposition 1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the function class of an
L
-layer neural network with:

1.

No biases

2.

1-Lipchitz activation functions (e.g., ReLU)

3.

Layer widths
d_{0},d_{1},\dots,d_{L}
where
d_{0}
is the input dimension

4.

Weight matrices
W^{l}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{l}\times d_{l-1}}
for
l=1,\dots,L

5.

Sparsity: each weight matrix
W^{l}
has a fixed sparsity pattern with at most
s_{l}=\rho_{l}d_{l}d_{l-1}
non-zero elements, where
\rho_{l}\ll 1

6.

Logits output:
f(\bm{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{C}},f\in\mathcal{F}
output logits for
N_{C}
classes

Moreover, assume the parameter space
\Theta
satisfies:

1.

Each layer has an initial weight matrix
W_{0}^{l}
with
\|W_{0}^{l}\|_{\text{op}}\leq 1

2.

The hypothesis space is constrained by
\|W^{l}-W_{0}^{l}\|_{\text{op}}\leq\beta
for each layer
l

3.

The input space is bounded:
\{x:\|x\|_{2}\leq B_{X}\}

Then, for any
\epsilon>0
, the covering number of
\mathcal{F}
under the supremum norm satisfies:

\log N(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim\left(\sum_{l=1}^{L}s_{l}\right)\cdot\log\left(\rho\right),

where

\|f(\bm{x})\|_{\infty}=\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{i\in[N_{C}]}|f_{i}(\bm{x})|

\rho=\frac{2\beta B_{X}\sqrt{L}(1+\beta)^{L-1}\max_{l}\sqrt{\min(d_{l},d_{l-1})}}{\epsilon}.

The proof is delayed to Sec.
H-D

The bound shows that the covering number depends on the total number of non-zero parameters
\sum s_{l}
, and the logarithmic term involves the network depth and width through
\max_{l}\sqrt{\min(d_{l},d_{l-1})}
. When sparsity is high (
\rho_{l}\ll 1
), the covering number is significantly reduced compared to the dense case.

H-B
3
CNNs

Long et al.
[
41
]
have demonstrated that the covering number of CNN models also follows the formulation in Equation (
30
). For more details, please refer to
[
41
]
.

H-B
4
LoRA and LoRA-LSF

For the hypothesis class of LoRA-LSF, denoted as
\mathcal{F}_{\text{LoRA}}
, as
proved in Claim 2 of our paper
, we have

\mathcal{N}_{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{\text{LoRA}},\varepsilon,M)\lesssim\bigl(\frac{R}{\varepsilon}\bigr)^{n_{L}\cdot n_{K}\cdot(Kr\cdot(m+n)-K^{2}r^{2})}

Thus, both LoRA and LoRA-LSF also satisfy the covering number assumption.

H-C

Conclusion

In summary, we establish several key results.
Lemma
14
shows that the covering number assumption follows from a general geometric fact about covering an
(\ell_{\infty})
ball in
\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
.
Next, Lemma
15
demonstrates that if a model’s
output
changes
Lipschitz-continuously
with respect to its
parameters
, then the function-class covering number is bounded by the covering number of the parameter ball, with the radius scaled by
L
.

We further prove that for
many common model classes
, including single-layer linear models, DNNs, CNNs, and LoRA/LoRA-LSF, the parameter-to-function mapping is Lipschitz on bounded sets.
Therefore, in all these cases, the covering number assumption holds:

\mathcal{N}_{\infty}(\mathcal{F},\varepsilon,M)\leq\bigl(r/\varepsilon\bigr)^{\nu}\

This analysis clarifies both
why the covering number assumption is reasonable
and
how it broadly applies
to these common model families.

H-D

Proofs

To prove the Prop.
1
, we need the following lemma about the Lipchitz constant for a NN defined above.

Lemma 16
.

For any neural network defined in Prop.
1
, its Lipchitz constant
L_{\theta}
can be upper bounded as follows:

\displaystyle L_{\theta}\leq B_{X}\cdot\sqrt{L}\cdot(1+\beta)^{L-1}.

Proof of Lem.
16
.

xxxx

The network has
L
layers, 1-Lipschitz activation functions (e.g., ReLU), and layer widths
d_{0},d_{1},\dots,d_{L}
. The parameter vector
\theta
contains all weight matrices. We aim to determine a Lipschitz constant
L_{\theta}
such that for any two parameter sets
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
:

\|f_{\theta}(x)-f_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)\|_{\infty}\leq L_{\theta}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2},

where
i
denotes the class channel,
\mathcal{X}=\{x:\|x\|_{2}\leq B_{X}\}
.

Since
\|f_{\theta}(x)-f_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)\|_{\infty}\leq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\|f_{\theta}(x)-f_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)\|_{2}
, it suffices to find
L_{\theta}
satisfying:

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\|f_{\theta}(x)-f_{\theta^{\prime}}(x)\|_{2}\leq L_{\theta}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2},

Step 1: Network Definition and Layer-wise Decomposition

Let
a_{0}=x
be the input, and for each layer
l=1,\dots,L
:

z_{l}=W^{l}a_{l-1},\quad a_{l}=\sigma_{l}(z_{l}),

where
\sigma_{l}
is 1-Lipschitz. The final output is
f_{\theta}(x)=a_{L}
.

Consider two parameter sets
\theta=(W^{1},\dots,W^{L})
and
\theta^{\prime}=(V^{1},\dots,V^{L})
. Define the intermediate activations:

a_{l}=\sigma_{l}(W^{l}a_{l-1}),~~a_{l}^{\prime}=\sigma_{l}(V^{l}a_{l-1}^{\prime}).

Let
\Delta_{l}=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\|a_{l}-a_{l}^{\prime}\|_{2}
. We will bound
\Delta_{L}
in terms of
\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}
.

Step 2: Recursive Bound on Activation Differences

For layer
l
, we have:

\|a_{l}-a_{l}^{\prime}\|_{2}=\|\sigma_{l}(W^{l}a_{l-1})-\sigma_{l}(V^{l}a_{l-1}^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq\|W^{l}a_{l-1}-V^{l}a_{l-1}^{\prime}\|_{2},

since
\sigma_{l}
is 1-Lipschitz. Then:

\|W^{l}a_{l-1}-V^{l}a_{l-1}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\|W^{l}a_{l-1}-W^{l}a_{l-1}^{\prime}\|_{2}+\|W^{l}a_{l-1}^{\prime}-V^{l}a_{l-1}^{\prime}\|_{2}.

The first term is bounded by
\|W^{l}\|_{\text{op}}\cdot\|a_{l-1}-a_{l-1}^{\prime}\|_{2}
, and the second term by
\|W^{l}-V^{l}\|_{\text{op}}\cdot\|a_{l-1}^{\prime}\|_{2}
. Thus:

\|a_{l}-a_{l}^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq\|W^{l}\|_{\text{op}}\cdot\|a_{l-1}-a_{l-1}^{\prime}\|_{2}+\|W^{l}-V^{l}\|_{\text{op}}\cdot\|a_{l-1}^{\prime}\|_{2}.

Taking supremum over
x
, we get:

\Delta_{l}\leq\|W^{l}\|_{\text{op}}\cdot\Delta_{l-1}+\|W^{l}-V^{l}\|_{\text{op}}\cdot A_{l-1},

where
A_{l-1}=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\|a_{l-1}^{\prime}\|_{2}
.

Step 3: Bounding the Norm of Activations

We now bound
A_{l}=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\|a_{l}\|_{2}
. Since
\sigma_{l}
is 1-Lipschitz and
\sigma_{l}(0)=0
(for ReLU), we have
\|a_{l}\|_{2}\leq\|z_{l}\|_{2}
. Then:

\|z_{l}\|_{2}=\|W^{l}a_{l-1}\|_{2}\leq\|W^{l}\|_{\text{op}}\|a_{l-1}\|_{2}.

Thus,
A_{l}\leq\|W^{l}\|_{\text{op}}A_{l-1}
. With
A_{0}=B_{X}
, we get:

A_{l}\leq B_{X}\prod_{j=1}^{l}\|W^{j}\|_{\text{op}}.

From the constraints,
\|W^{j}\|_{\text{op}}\leq\|W_{0}^{j}\|_{\text{op}}+\beta\leq 1+\beta
, so:

A_{l}\leq B_{X}(1+\beta)^{l}.

Similarly, for the network with parameters
\theta^{\prime}
,
A_{l}^{\prime}\leq B_{X}(1+\beta)^{l}
.

Step 4: Solving the Recursion for
\Delta_{L}

We have:

\Delta_{l}\leq(1+\beta)\Delta_{l-1}+\|W^{l}-V^{l}\|_{\text{op}}B_{X}(1+\beta)^{l-1}.

Unrolling the recursion:

\Delta_{L}\leq B_{X}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\|W^{l}-V^{l}\|_{\text{op}}(1+\beta)^{L-1}.

Step 5: Relating to Parameter Difference

The parameter difference is:

\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\|W^{l}-V^{l}\|_{F}^{2}.

We have
\|W^{l}-V^{l}\|_{\text{op}}\leq\|W^{l}-V^{l}\|_{F}
, so:

\sum_{l=1}^{L}\|W^{l}-V^{l}\|_{\text{op}}\leq\sqrt{L}\sqrt{\sum_{l=1}^{L}\|W^{l}-V^{l}\|_{F}^{2}}=\sqrt{L}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}.

Thus:

\Delta_{L}\leq B_{X}(1+\beta)^{L-1}\sqrt{L}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}.

Therefore, the Lipschitz constant
L_{\theta}
satisfies:

L_{\theta}\leq B_{X}\cdot\sqrt{L}\cdot(1+\beta)^{L-1}.

∎

Proof of Prop.
1
.

xxxx

Step 1: Parameter Space Decomposition

Note that the hypothesis space
\mathcal{F}
consists of neural networks with different sparsity patterns. For each layer
l
, there are
\binom{d_{l}d_{l-1}}{s_{l}}
possible sparsity patterns (choices of which
s_{l}
elements are non-zero).

Let
\mathcal{S}_{l}
be the set of all possible support sets (positions of non-zero elements) for layer
l
, with
|\mathcal{S}_{l}|=\binom{d_{l}d_{l-1}}{s_{l}}
.

The overall parameter space can be decomposed as:

\Theta=\bigcup_{S_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1},\dots,S_{L}\in\mathcal{S}_{L}}\Theta_{S_{1},\dots,S_{L}}

where
\Theta_{S_{1},\dots,S_{L}}
is the set of parameters with fixed sparsity pattern
(S_{1},\dots,S_{L})
.

Step 2: Covering Each Fixed Sparsity Pattern

For a fixed sparsity pattern
(S_{1},\dots,S_{L})
, the parameter space
\Theta_{S_{1},\dots,S_{L}}
has dimension
\sum_{l=1}^{L}s_{l}
.

From the spectral norm constraint
\|W^{l}-W_{0}^{l}\|_{\text{op}}\leq\beta
and the fact that

\|A\|_{F}\leq\mathsf{rank}(A)\cdot\|A\|_{\text{op}}\leq\sqrt{\min(d_{l},d_{l-1})}\|A\|_{\text{op}}

we have:

\|W^{l}-W_{0}^{l}\|_{F}\leq\beta\sqrt{\min(d_{l},d_{l-1})}.

Since only the
s_{l}
non-zero elements contribute to the Frobenius norm, this implies that the vector of non-zero parameters
\theta^{l}
lies in a ball of radius
R_{l}=\beta\sqrt{\min(d_{l},d_{l-1})}
in
\mathbb{R}^{s_{l}}
.

Therefore, for a fixed sparsity pattern, the parameter space is contained in a product of balls:

\Theta_{S_{1},\dots,S_{L}}\subseteq\prod_{l=1}^{L}B_{R_{l}}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{\sum_{l=1}^{L}s_{l}}

where
B_{R_{l}}(0)
is the ball of radius
R_{l}
in
\mathbb{R}^{s_{l}}
.

Step 3: Lipchitz Constant (Same as Before)

From the previous derivation, we have:

\|f_{\theta}-f_{\theta^{\prime}}\|_{\infty}\leq L_{\theta}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}

where
L_{\theta}=B_{X}\sqrt{L}(1+\beta)^{L-1}
.

Step 4: Covering Number for Fixed Sparsity Pattern

For a fixed sparsity pattern, the covering number satisfies:

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{F}_{S_{1},\dots,S_{L}},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq N\left(\frac{\epsilon}{L_{\theta}},\Theta_{S_{1},\dots,S_{L}},\|\cdot\|_{2}\right)

where
\mathcal{F}_{S_{1},\dots,S_{L}}
is the function class with fixed sparsity pattern.

Since
\Theta_{S_{1},\dots,S_{L}}\subseteq B_{R}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{\sum s_{l}}
where
R=\sqrt{\sum_{l=1}^{L}R_{l}^{2}}\leq\sqrt{L}\max_{l}R_{l}
, we have:

N\left(\delta,\Theta_{S_{1},\dots,S_{L}},\|\cdot\|_{2}\right)\leq\left(1+\frac{2R}{\delta}\right)^{\sum s_{l}}

Setting
\delta=\epsilon/L_{\theta}
:

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{F}_{S_{1},\dots,S_{L}},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq\left(1+\frac{2RL_{\theta}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\sum s_{l}}

Substituting
R\leq\sqrt{L}\max_{l}\beta\sqrt{\min(d_{l},d_{l-1})}
and
L_{\theta}=B_{X}\sqrt{L}(1+\beta)^{L-1}
:

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{F}_{S_{1},\dots,S_{L}},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq\left(1+\frac{2\beta B_{X}\sqrt{L}(1+\beta)^{L-1}\max_{l}\sqrt{\min(d_{l},d_{l-1})}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\sum s_{l}}

Step 5: Union Bound Over All Sparsity Patterns

Since there are
\prod_{l=1}^{L}\binom{d_{l}d_{l-1}}{s_{l}}
total sparsity patterns, we have:

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq\left[\prod_{l=1}^{L}\binom{d_{l}d_{l-1}}{s_{l}}\right]\cdot\left(1+\frac{2\beta B_{X}\sqrt{L}(1+\beta)^{L-1}\max_{l}\sqrt{\min(d_{l},d_{l-1})}}{\epsilon}\right)^{\sum s_{l}}

\rho=\frac{2\beta B_{X}\sqrt{L}(1+\beta)^{L-1}\max_{l}\sqrt{\min(d_{l},d_{l-1})}}{\epsilon}

Taking logarithms:

\log N(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq\sum_{l=1}^{L}\log\binom{d_{l}d_{l-1}}{s_{l}}+\left(\sum_{l=1}^{L}s_{l}\right)\cdot\log\left(1+\rho\right)

Using the bound
\binom{n}{k}\leq\left(\frac{en}{k}\right)^{k}
, we get:

\displaystyle\log N(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{L}\log\left(\frac{ed_{l}d_{l-1}}{s_{l}}\right)+\left(\sum_{l=1}^{L}s_{l}\right)\cdot\log\left(1+\rho\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sum_{l=1}^{L}s_{l}\right)\cdot\log\left(\rho\right)

This completes the proof.

∎

Appendix I
Discussion on the Dependency of the Generalization Bound on
C

In this paper, our goal is to sharpen the bound w.r.t the number of samples
M,N
. We do not improve the bound over the number of classes
C
, since typically for large-scale datasets
C\ll\min\{M,N\}
.

In this paper, we employ covering number based technique to derive the bound. The dependency on
C
is implicitly contained in the metric entropy,
i.e.
, the logarithm of the covering number. In this sense, we now provide the bound for metric entropy in the new version and analyze the dependency over
C
.

I-A

Covering Number Derivation and Dependency on
C
in Mixture-of-Experts Models

Note that we use mixture-of-experts (MoE) models to address test-agnostic long-tail recognition. Specifically, the entire network can be viewed as a composition of a shared backbone
G
and
K
expert-specific modules
F_{i}
, where each expert
F_{i}
is responsible for a specific meta-distribution. Formally, each expert outputs its logits through the composition
f_{i}\circ g
.

This structure naturally induces a
product space
of expert function classes (
F=F_{1}\times\cdots\times F_{K}
), combined with a shared network for latent embedding (
G
). Consequently, the overall function class
F\circ G
involves both
composition
(due to hierarchical structure) and
product
(due to multiple experts). This dual structure leads to a
more complex but expressive
form of the resulting covering number bound:

Proposition 2

(Covering Number Bound for Composite MLP Space with Spectral Norm Constraints)
.

Let
G
be an
L_{1}
-layer MLP which satisfies the assumptions in Prop.
1
. Specifically, the layer widths are defined as
k_{1},k_{2}\cdots,k_{L_{1}}
, respectively. For the
\ell-th
layer, there are only
g_{l}
nonzero elements in the weight matrix.

Let
F=F_{1}\times\cdots\times F_{K}
be a product space for multi-expert neural networks, where each
F_{i}
is an
L_{2}
-layer MLP. Each
F_{i}
satisfies all the assumptions in Prop.
1
.
We assume that all
F_{i}
s share the same layer widths denoted by
d_{1},\dots,d_{L_{2}}
. Moreover, for each
i
, there are at most
s_{l}
non-zero elements for the weight matrix of the
\ell
-th layer.

Then, for any
\epsilon>0
, the covering number of
F\circ G
under the supremum norm satisfies:

\log N(\epsilon,F\circ G,\|\cdot\|_{\infty,M})\lesssim\left(\sum_{l=1}^{L_{1}}g_{l}\right)\cdot\log\left(\rho_{G}\right)+K\cdot\left(\sum_{l=1}^{L_{2}}s_{l}\right)\cdot\log\left(\rho_{F}\right)

where:

\forall f\in F,~~~\|f\|_{\infty,M}=\max_{i\in[K]}\max_{j\in[N_{C}]}\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|f_{\theta,j}^{(i)}(x)|,

f_{\theta,j}^{(i)}(x)
is
j
-th class logit for the
i
-th expert, and

\displaystyle\rho_{F}=\frac{4\beta B_{X}^{2}\sqrt{L_{1}\cdot L_{2}}(1+\beta)^{L_{1}+L_{2}-2}\max_{l}\sqrt{\min(d_{l},d_{l-1})}}{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\rho_{G}=\frac{4\sqrt{k_{L_{1}}}\beta B_{X}^{3}L_{1}\sqrt{L_{2}}(1+\beta)^{2L_{1}+L_{2}-3}\max_{l}\sqrt{\min(k_{l},k_{l-1})}}{\epsilon}

The proof is delayed to the
Proofs
below.

From Proposition.
2
, the dependency on the
number of classes
C
arises
naturally through the
last layer
of each expert network. The only function of
C
is
s_{L_{2}}
. In particular, the sparsity term
s_{L_{2}}
in the bound corresponds to the number of non-zero elements in the weight matrix of the final layer, whose output dimension is
C
.
Therefore,
s_{L_{2}}
is approximately proportional to
C
, leading to a
linear dependency
of the overall covering number and hence the generalization bound on
C
.

However, since
s_{L_{2}}
contributes only a single term in the total sparsity sum
\sum_{l=1}^{L_{2}}s_{l}
, the impact of
C
is relatively small when earlier layers dominate the parameter count. Moreover, in practice, the number of sampled test distributions
M
and the training sample size
N
are typically much larger than
C
(e.g., in ImageNet-LT,
C=1000
, while
M
and
N
can be tens of thousands).
Therefore, while
C
appears linearly in theory, it has a minor impact on the overall bound. Our primary contribution lies in achieving tight bounds with respect to
M
and
N
.

I-B

Avoiding Dependency on
C
in Section 6.3 (Fine-Tuning Foundation Models)

In Section 6.3 of the main paper, we analyze the generalization bounds of DirMixE with Latent Skill Fine-tuning (LSF). Our formulation effectively decouples the bound from
C
by leveraging parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) and Taylor expansion.

Key Steps:

•

Local Linearized Assumption based on Taylor Expansion
: We approximate the logit function
f^{(i)}_{\Theta^{(i)}}(x)
by its first-order Taylor expansion around a reference point
\Theta^{(i),*}
(Eqs. 12 and 13):

\displaystyle f^{(i)}_{\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}}(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle f^{(i)}_{\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}}(\bm{x})+\langle\nabla_{\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}}f^{(i)}_{\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}}(\bm{x}),\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}-\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}\rangle+r(i,\bm{x})

(32)

Here

r(i,\bm{x})=\sup_{\theta\in\mathsf{LinSeg}}(\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}-\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star})^{\top}\bm{H}_{\theta}(\bm{x})(\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}-\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}),

(33)

where
\mathsf{LinSeg}
denotes the set of all linear segments between
\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}
and
\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}
, while
\bm{H}_{\theta}(\bm{x})
is the corresponding Hessian matrix.

where
\Delta\Theta^{(i)}
is the incremental parameter for task
i
, and
\mathcal{R}(i,x)
captures higher-order residuals (Hessian information). In this paper, we employ the
\delta
-compact assumption to choose reference points dynamically to ensure the approximation precision and consistency with each
\epsilon
-ball.
Please see the response 2-1 for more details about the assumption.
Based on this linearized logit function, for two distinct functions
f,\tilde{f}
, we have:

|f^{(i)}_{\Theta^{(i)}}(x)-\tilde{f}^{(i)}_{\Theta^{(i)}}(x)|\leq L\cdot\|\Delta\Theta^{(i)}-{\Delta}\tilde{\Theta}^{(i)}\|.

Then, according to Lem.
15
in the response, we know that the covering number for the overall model class can be upper bounded by the covering number of the class for
\Delta\Theta^{(i)}
, i.e., the trainable parameters.

In PEFT, the pre-trained foundation model (e.g., CLIP) is kept frozen, and only a small number of low-rank incremental parameters (e.g., via LoRA or Adapter) are updated. Crucially, the output layer (classifier) remains fixed, either frozen or initialized by the pre-trained text encoder, thus its complexity does not affect the overall trainable parameters space.

•

Covering Number for Incremental Parameters
: The hypothesis class for incremental parameters is defined based on low-rank structures. For example, in LoRA-LSF, we have
\Delta\Theta=AB^{\top}
with
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times r}
,
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
, with rank
r\ll\min(m,n)
. The resulting covering number depends only on
m,n,r,K
and the number of layers
n_{L}
, but not on
C
. Specifically, as shown in Appendix E.3, we have:

\log\mathcal{N}_{\infty}(\mathcal{F}^{\text{LoRA}},\epsilon,n)\lesssim n_{L}\cdot n_{K}\cdot(Kr\cdot(m+n-Kr))\log\left(\frac{L_{f}n_{L}R}{\epsilon}\right),

where
\mathcal{F}^{\text{LoRA}}
denotes the function class of networks with LoRA modules,
n_{K}
is the number of sampled meta-distributions, and
L_{f}
is the Lipschitz constant with respect to the parameters.

This covering number bound is clearly independent of
C
. A similar conclusion holds for Adapter-LSF, whose bound scales as
2n_{L}n_{K}(m+n-r)\log(L_{f}n_{L}R/\epsilon)
, also free from
C
-dependence.

Consequently, in Theorem 4, the generalization bound for LSF does not explicitly depend on
C
, as the covering number and Lipschitz constant are derived solely from the incremental parameters. This is a key innovation of our analysis for foundation models.

I-C

Proofs

Proof of Prop.
2
.

xxxxxx

From Lem.
19
, we have:

\displaystyle N(\epsilon,F\circ G,\|\cdot\|_{\infty,M})\leq\left[\prod_{i=1}^{K}N\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2},F_{i},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\right)\right]\cdot N\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2L_{F}},G,\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\right),

(34)

For each of the covering numbers on the right-hand side, we can directly employ Prop.
1
to derive the bound. For each
F_{i}
, the input becomes the output of the functions in
G
. Hence, we need to bound the norm of the outputs in G. Since
x\in\mathcal{X}
is bounded, we only need to upper bound the Lip. Constant of
G
as
L_{G}
and then get
B_{G}\leq L_{G}\cdot B_{X}
. The upper bound of the Lip. constant of
G
follows Lem.
16
. This results in the following Lip. bound for
G
:

L_{G}\leq B_{X}\sqrt{L_{1}}(1+\beta)^{L_{1}-1}.

In this sense, we have:

B_{G}\leq L_{G}B_{X}\leq B_{X}^{2}\sqrt{L_{1}}(1+\beta)^{L_{1}-1}.

This means that the Lip. for
F_{i}
becomes:

L_{F}\leq B_{G}\sqrt{L_{2}}(1+\beta)^{L_{2}-1}\leq B_{X}^{2}\sqrt{L_{1}\cdot L_{2}}(1+\beta)^{L_{1}+L_{2}-2}.

From Prop.
1
, we have:

\log\left(N\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2},F_{i},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\right)\right)\lesssim\left(\sum_{l=1}^{L_{2}}s_{l}\right)\cdot\log\left(\rho_{F}\right),

where

\rho_{F}=\frac{4\beta B_{X}^{2}\sqrt{L_{1}\cdot L_{2}}(1+\beta)^{L_{1}+L_{2}-2}\max_{l}\sqrt{\min(d_{l},d_{l-1})}}{\epsilon}

Moreover, combining the upper bound of
L_{F},L_{G}
and the proof of Prop.
1
, we reach an upper bound for the metric entropy for
G
:

\log\left(N\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2L_{F}\sqrt{k_{L_{1}}}},G,\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\right)\right)\lesssim\left(\sum_{l=1}^{L_{1}}g_{l}\right)\cdot\log\left(\rho_{G}\right),

where

\displaystyle\rho_{G}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\beta B_{X}\sqrt{L_{1}}(1+\beta)^{L_{1}-1}\max_{l}\sqrt{\min(k_{l},k_{l-1})}}{\epsilon/(L_{F}\sqrt{k_{L_{1}}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\sqrt{k_{L_{1}}}\beta B_{X}^{3}L_{1}\sqrt{L_{2}}(1+\beta)^{2L_{1}+L_{2}-3}\max_{l}\sqrt{\min(k_{l},k_{l-1})}}{\epsilon}

Combining the upper bounds with Eq.
34
, we complete the proof.

∎

Lemma 17
.

Consider two hypothesis spaces
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}
, and their composition:

\mathcal{H}\circ\mathcal{G}=\left\{\text{The set of}~N_{C}~\text{class logits }f:f=h\circ g,h\in\mathcal{H},~g\in\mathcal{G},f_{i}~~\text{is i-th class channel output}\right\}.

We have the following upper bound for the covering number w.r.t the infinity norm:

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{H}\circ\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq N\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2},\mathcal{H},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\right)\times N\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2L_{H}\sqrt{d}},\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\right).

where

L_{H}
is the uniform Lipschitz constant for the elements in
\mathcal{H}
and
d
is the output dimension of
g\in\mathcal{G}

Proof of Lem.
17
.

The following proof is based on constructing an
\epsilon
-cover for
H\circ G
.

Step 1 Covering Precision Selection

Because
H
is Lipschitz continuous, we adjust the covering precision for
G
and
H
. Let:

\displaystyle\delta=\frac{\epsilon}{2L_{H}},\eta=\frac{\epsilon}{2}

Step 2 Constructing the Covering Set

1.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\text{cover}}
be a
\delta
-cover set of
G
under
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, with size
N_{G}=N(\delta,G,\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
.

2.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\text{cover}}
be an
\eta
-cover set of
H
under
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, with size
N_{H}=N(\eta,H,\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
.

3.

Define the composite covering set
\mathcal{C}=\{h^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime}:h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\text{cover}},g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{\text{cover}}\}
. Clearly,
\mathcal{C}\subseteq H\circ G
and
|\mathcal{C}|\leq N_{H}\times N_{G}
.

Step 3 Verifying
\mathcal{C}
is an
\epsilon
-cover

1.

Take any
f=h\circ g\in\mathcal{H}\circ\mathcal{G}
, where
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
g\in\mathcal{G}
.

2.

Because
\mathcal{G}_{\text{cover}}
is a
\delta
-cover of
G
, there exists
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{\text{cover}}
such that
\|g-g^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\delta
.

3.

Because
\mathcal{H}_{\text{cover}}
is an
\eta
-cover of
H
, there exists
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\text{cover}}
such that
\|h-h^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
.

4.

Consider
f^{\prime}=h^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
. For any
x\in X
:

\left\|{f(x)-f^{\prime}(x)}\right\|_{\infty}=\left\|{h(g(x))-h^{\prime}(g^{\prime}(x))}\right\|_{\infty}.

Decompose this expression:

\left\|{h(g(x))-h^{\prime}(g^{\prime}(x))}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|{h(g(x))-h(g^{\prime}(x))}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|{h(g^{\prime}(x))-h^{\prime}(g^{\prime}(x))}\right\|_{\infty}.

5.

Analyze the first term: Because elements of
\mathcal{H}
are Lipschitz continuous,

\displaystyle\left\|{h(g(x))-h(g^{\prime}(x))}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq L_{H}\cdot\sqrt{d}\cdot\left\|{g(x)-g^{\prime}(x)}\right\|_{\infty}\leq L_{H}\sqrt{d}\|g-g^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq L_{H}\cdot\sqrt{d}\cdot\delta=L_{H}\cdot\sqrt{d}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{2L_{H}\sqrt{d}}=\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

6.

Analyze the second term:

\left\|{h(g^{\prime}(x))-h^{\prime}(g^{\prime}(x))}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\|h-h^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\eta=\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

7.

Therefore,

\left\|{f(x)-f^{\prime}(x)}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}=\epsilon.

Since this holds for all
x\in X
, we have
\|f-f^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon
.

Conclusion

\mathcal{C}
is an
\epsilon
-cover for
\mathcal{H}\circ\mathcal{G}
, therefore:

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{H}\circ\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq N\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2},\mathcal{H},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\right)\times N\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2L_{H}\sqrt{d}},\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\right).

∎

Lemma 18
.

Consider a function space
F=F_{1}\times F_{2}\times\cdots\times F_{K}
as a product space for K-expert neural networks, where
F_{1},F_{2},\cdots,F_{K}
are the corresponding experts. For any
f=(f^{(1)},f^{(2)},\cdots,f^{(K)})\in F
, its norm is defined as:

\|f\|_{\infty,M}=\max_{i=1,\cdots,K}\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}.

Note that each
f^{(i)}
is an expert NN model which outputs logits for
N_{C}
classes.

Then we have the following upper bound for the covering number:

N(\epsilon,F,\|\cdot\|_{\infty,M})\leq\prod_{i=1}^{K}N(\epsilon,F_{i},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}).

Proof of Lem.
18
.

xxxxxx

Step 1 Define the Covering Set:

1.

For each
i=1,2,\cdots,K
, let
\mathcal{C}_{i}
be an
\epsilon
-cover of
F_{i}
under the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, meaning for any
f^{(i)}\in F_{i}
, there exists
c_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
such that
\|f^{(i)}-c_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon
. The covering number size is
N_{i}=N(\epsilon,F_{i},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
.

2.

Define the product covering set
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\times\mathcal{C}_{2}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{K}
, i.e., each element
c\in\mathcal{C}
is a
K
-tuple
c=(c_{1},c_{2},\cdots,c_{K})
where
c_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
. Clearly,
\mathcal{C}\subseteq F
, and
|\mathcal{C}|=\prod_{i=1}^{K}|\mathcal{C}_{i}|=\prod_{i=1}^{K}N(\epsilon,F_{i},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
.

Step 2 Verify
\mathcal{C}
is an
\epsilon
-cover for
F

1.

Take any
f=(f^{(1)},f^{(2)},\cdots,f^{(K)})\in F
.

2.

For each
i
, since
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is an
\epsilon
-cover of
F_{i}
, there exists
c_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
such that
\|f^{(i)}-c_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon
.

3.

Let
c=(c_{1},c_{2},\cdots,c_{K})\in\mathcal{C}
. Then for each
i
,
\|f^{(i)}-c_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon
.

4.

Therefore, according to the definition of the norm on
F
:

\|f-c\|_{\infty,M}=\max_{i=1,\cdots,K}\|f^{(i)}-c_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon.

5.

This shows that for any
f\in F
, there exists
c\in\mathcal{C}
such that
\|f-c\|\leq\epsilon
, so
\mathcal{C}
is an
\epsilon
-cover for
F
.

Step 3 Upper Bound on Covering Number

Since
\mathcal{C}
is an
\epsilon
-cover for
F
and
|\mathcal{C}|=\prod_{i=1}^{K}N(\epsilon,F_{i},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
, it follows that:

N(\epsilon,F,\|\cdot\|_{\infty,M})\leq\prod_{i=1}^{K}N(\epsilon,F_{i},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}).

According to the proof of Lem.
17
, we have:

N(\epsilon,F_{i},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq N(\epsilon,F_{i},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}).

Then the proof is completed.
∎

Lemma 19
.

We consider the composite space
F\circ G
, where
F
is the product space
F_{1}\times F_{2}\times\cdots\times F_{K}
in Lem.
18
. The space
G
is another function class. The composite space
F\circ G
is defined as
F\circ G=\{f\circ g:f\in F,g\in G\}
, where
f\circ g(x)=f(g(x))
.

Then we have the following inequality:

N(\epsilon,F\circ G,\|\cdot\|_{\infty,M})\leq\left[\prod_{i=1}^{K}N\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2},F_{i},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\right)\right]\cdot N\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2L_{F}},G,\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\right),

where elements in
\mathcal{F}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{F}_{K}
are all
L_{F}
-Lip w.r.t.
\ell_{2}
norm.

Proof of Lem.
19
.

The proof follows Lem.
17
and Lem.
18
directly.
∎

Appendix J
Validity of the Exponential-tail Loss Decay Assumption

Below, we validate the assumption in Col.
1
. Specifically, we validate the assumption for
1) per-sample losses
,

\mathbb{P}_{x}[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{E}}[\ell(f(x),y,P_{i})]\geq\tau]\lesssim\exp(-\lambda_{1}\tau),\quad\text{for}\ \tau>\tau_{0}

and for
2) per-distribution loss
:

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{E}}[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}}[\ell(f(x),y,P_{i})]\geq\tau]\lesssim\exp(-\lambda_{2}\tau),\quad\text{for}\ \tau>\tau_{0}

For the right-hand side cumulative distribution function (CDF)
\mathbb{P}(\tau)=\exp(-\lambda\tau),\ \tau>\tau_{0}
, its corresponding probability density function (PDF) follows the form:

p(\tau)=\lambda e^{-\lambda\tau},\text{for}\ \tau>\tau_{0}

Accordingly, we use the cumulative distribution function (CDF) and its associated PDF to fit the empirical loss distributions and assess the validity of the assumption.

To verify this assumption in practice, we conduct experiments on
CIFAR-100-LT and ImageNet-LT
. From the results, we observe that both distributions demonstrate approximately exponential decay in their upper tails. This provides strong empirical support for the assumptions underlying Corollary 1 and suggests that the exponential-tail characterization is reasonable in practice. We will present the detailed empirical validation below.

1) Validation of the per-sample tail assumption.

Fig. 9:

Empirical validation of per-sample loss exponential-tail behavior.

(a)
Loss histogram and exponential PDF fit on CIFAR-100-LT.

(b)
Loss histogram and exponential PDF fit on ImageNet-LT.

(c)
Variation of the coefficient
c
with respect to
\alpha
for CIFAR-100-LT and ImageNet-LT.

For each training example
x
, we compute its averaged loss

\bar{\ell}(x)=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\ell\!\left(f(x),y,P_{i}\right),

and use the resulting empirical distribution
\{\bar{\ell}(x):x\in S\}
to evaluate tail behavior.

To test the exponential-tail hypothesis, we examine the survival function

F(\tau)=\mathbb{P}[\bar{\ell}(x)\geq\tau]

and perform
log-linear regression
by fitting
\log F(\tau)
against
\tau
. Under an exponential tail, we expect

\log F(\tau)=-\lambda_{1}\tau+\text{const}

The fitted model achieves a
high
R^{2}
value of
0.95
on CIFAR-100-LT and
0.94
on ImageNet-LT
, indicating a strong linear relationship after the log transform and suggesting that the upper tail of the empirical loss distribution closely matches exponential decay. We further visualize the empirical histogram together with the fitted exponential PDF curve (
\lambda_{1}e^{-\lambda_{1}\tau}
) for CIFAR-100-LT and ImageNet-LT datasets in Fig.
9
-(a) and Fig.
9
-(b). The figures demonstrate that the observed tail trend aligns well with the exponential form. These results provide compelling evidence supporting the per-sample exponential-tail assumption.

The estimated decay parameter is
\lambda_{1}=0.758
for CIFAR-100-LT and
\lambda_{1}=0.581
for ImageNet-LT. Our theory suggests that
\lambda_{1}
should scale as
\lambda_{1}\asymp\log(N)\cdot N^{1/\alpha}
, meaning that there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\lambda_{1}=c\cdot\log(N)\cdot N^{1/\alpha}.

Using the empirical estimate of
\lambda_{1}
, we compute the implied coefficient
c
and plot its variation with respect to
\alpha
in Fig.
9
-(c). We observe that the estimated coefficient
c
consistently remains above approximately
0.05
when
\alpha>10
for CIFAR-100-LT and ImageNet-LT, consistent with the theoretical constraint that
\lambda_{1}
and
\log(N)\cdot N^{1/\alpha}

are of the same order
.

2) Validation of the per-distribution tail assumption.

For each sampled label distribution
P_{i}
, we average the losses over the dataset:

\tilde{\ell}(P_{i})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{(x,y)\in S}\ell\left(f(x),y,P_{i}\right).

We then analyze the empirical distribution
\{\tilde{\ell}(P_{i}):i=1,\dots,M\}
to evaluate the tail behavior for per-distribution losses.

Similar to the per-sample analysis, we investigate whether the distribution of
\tilde{\ell}(P_{i})
exhibits exponential decay in its upper tail. Specifically, we validate this by applying a similar log–linear regression procedure, fitting
\log\mathbb{P}[\tilde{\ell}(P_{i})\geq\tau]
against
\tau
.

Fig. 10:

Empirical validation of per-distribution loss exponential-tail behavior.

(a)
Loss histogram and exponential PDF fit on CIFAR-100-LT.

(b)
Loss histogram and exponential PDF fit on ImageNet-LT.

(c)
Variation of the coefficient
c
with respect to
\alpha
for CIFAR-100-LT and ImageNet-LT.

The fitted model achieves a
R^{2}
value of
0.70
on CIFAR-100-LT and
0.95
on ImageNet-LT
. The visualization of the empirical histogram, along with the fitted exponential PDF curve, is shown in Fig.
10
-(a) and Fig.
10
-(b). Notably, compared with the per-sample case, the exponential fit near the lower-loss region is less precise. This behavior is expected: per-distribution losses are averaged across many samples and therefore rarely approach zero—in fact, the minimum value of
\tilde{\ell}(P_{i})
is strictly positive in our experiments because some samples are inevitably misclassified under each distribution. However, this deviation at small loss values is
not problematic
for our theory. The exponential-tail assumption is only required to hold for
sufficiently large
\tau
. In the high-loss regime, the fitted exponential curve aligns well with the empirical decay as shown in the results. Thus, the behavior of the upper tail remains consistent with our assumption, validating the applicability of the assumption in the per-distribution setting.

The estimated decay parameter is
\lambda_{2}=1.599
for CIFAR-100-LT and
\lambda_{2}=1.021
for ImageNet-LT. To further support the same-order scaling relationship, we repeat the above procedure and compute the implied coefficient

c
for varying values of
\alpha
in the per-distribution setting, as shown in Fig.
10
-(c). In particular, when
\alpha>8
, the estimated coefficient
c
consistently remains above approximately
0.05
across both CIFAR-100-LT and ImageNet-LT. This empirical finding again aligns with the theoretical constraint that
\lambda_{2}
and
\log(M)\cdot M^{1/\alpha}
are of the same order.

Appendix K
Validity of the Lipschitz Continuous Assumption

Thank you for your question! We would like to clarify that the loss function considered in our analysis is actually the composition of the cross-entropy loss and the softmax function, i.e.,
\ell=\ell_{\mathrm{CE}}\circ\mathsf{softmax}
, where the remaining part corresponds to the logits
f(x)
. Under the composition
\ell
is Lip. Continuous. The earlier version of the manuscript misstated this point, and we will revise Assumption 1 accordingly to make this explicit. The detailed adjustment is described below.

We show below that the
\ell_{\text{CE}}\circ\mathsf{softmax}
is
Lipschitz continuous
with respect to the logits
z
under the infinity norm, which addresses the concern about the
validity of the assumption
raised in the question.

Let
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the logits,
y
the one-hot label, and
p=\mathrm{softmax}(z)
. The standard cross-entropy loss is

L(z)=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}\log p_{i},\qquad p_{i}=\frac{e^{z_{i}}}{\sum_{j}e^{z_{j}}}.

Its gradient with respect to
z
is:

\nabla_{z}L=p-y.

The
\ell_{1}
norm of the gradient, which corresponds to the Lipschitz constant under
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
, is

\|\nabla_{z}L\|_{1}=\sum_{j}|p_{j}-y_{j}|=2(1-p_{k}),

where
k
is the index of the true class (
y_{k}=1
). Since
p_{k}\in(0,1]
, the supremum of
\|\nabla_{z}L\|_{1}
is
2
as
p_{k}\to 0
. Therefore,

|L(z_{1})-L(z_{2})|\leq 2\|z_{1}-z_{2}\|_{\infty},

which shows that the cross-entropy loss is
2
-Lipschitz
with respect to the logits.

In summary, the composed loss is Lipschitz continuous under the infinity norm as a function of the logits, and its Lipschitz constant is bounded by
2
. Hence, Assumption 1.1 is both mild and valid.

Appendix L
More Discussion About Definition
1
-
3

L-A

Def.
1
: Avoiding Uniform Bounds and Focusing on ”Good Regions”

In generalization analysis, traditional uniform bounds (e.g., those based on VC-dimension or Rademacher complexity) can be loose because they consider the entire hypothesis space, including poorly performing models. However, in practice, we are only interested in well-trained models that exhibit good generalization behavior. Definition 1 is designed to narrow down the hypothesis space to these ”good regions” where the model performs well consistently.

There are two subsets to capture ”good regions”:

A^{I}
represents the set where, for a given sample, the loss is small with high probability across different test distributions.

A^{M}
represents the set where, for a fixed test distribution, the overall expected risk is small with high probability. This captures the model’s average performance on the target distribution.

The intuition here is that a well-trained model should have a high probability of lying within these two sets. By intersecting the original hypothesis space with the high probability sets, we restrict our analysis to these models correspondingly. This approach avoids the pessimism of uniform bounds and allows for tighter generalization guarantees in practical scenarios where models are already well-optimized.

L-B

Def.
2
&
3
: Linear Approximation and Covering Number Bound

Def.
2
introduces the Voronoi diagram, a well-known mathematical tool
[
62
,
63
,
64
,
65
,
66
]
. But Def.
3
is new and specific to our problem.

Definitions
2
and
3
together enable a linear approximation of the model’s logits around local minima, which is crucial for deriving covering-number-based bounds. Specifically, Eq. (
35
) approximate the logits by expanding around the nearest local minimum. Here we repeat it for clarity.

\displaystyle f^{(i)}_{\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}}(\bm{x})=

\displaystyle f^{(i)}_{\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}}(\bm{x})+\langle\nabla_{\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}}f^{(i)}_{\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}}(\bm{x}),\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}-\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}\rangle+r(i,\bm{x})

(35)

Here

r(i,\bm{x})=\sup_{\theta\in\mathsf{LinSeg}}(\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}-\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star})^{\top}\bm{H}_{\theta}(\bm{x})(\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}-\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}),

(36)

where
\mathsf{LinSeg}
denotes the set of all linear segments between
\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}
and
\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}
, while
\bm{H}_{\theta}(\bm{x})
is the corresponding Hessian matrix.

The theoretical arguments are based on covering sets for the parameter space, each of which is a small open ball. To integrate the simplicity of linear approximation, we need to ensure that the same local minimum
\Delta\Theta^{\star}
is chosen within a specific ball of the covering set.

Specifically, we can rule out the effect of residue
r(i,\bm{x})
through a Lip. based inequality on the loss function (see Proof of Thm.
4
). So, for two parameters
\Delta\Theta
and
\Delta\tilde{\Theta}
, if their local solutions are consistent, we have the bound:

\displaystyle|f^{(i)}_{\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}}(\bm{x})+\langle\nabla_{\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}}f^{(i)}_{\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}}(\bm{x}),\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}-\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}\rangle-f^{(i)}_{\bm{\tilde{\Theta}}^{(i),\star}}(\bm{x})+\langle\nabla_{\bm{\tilde{\Theta}}^{(i),\star}}f^{(i)}_{\bm{\tilde{\Theta}}^{(i),\star}}(\bm{x}),\Delta\bm{\tilde{\Theta}}^{(i)}-\Delta\bm{\tilde{\Theta}}^{(i),\star}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq L\cdot\|\Delta\bm{\Theta}^{(i)}-\Delta\bm{\tilde{\Theta}}^{(i)}\|_{F}

Then the covering number of the linearized logits could be upper-bounded by that of the incremental parameter space.

Def.
2
defines a Voronoi diagram, which partitions the parameter space into three cells
\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},\mathcal{V}_{3}
. The nearest local minimum is the same for all parameters within a cell. In other words, within a cell, the model’s behavior can be characterized by a fixed local minimum, allowing us to use linear approximations uniformly. Based
on this definition, to ensure
\bm{\Theta}^{(i),\star}
is the nearest local solution within the small covering ball, we only need to ensure that the ball does not cross the border of the cells. This is precisely the meaning of Def.
3
.

According to Fig.
5
, Def.
3
states that all the elements should be at least
\delta
away from the boundary cross cells. By choosing
\delta
as the diameter of the balls in the covering set. We can then ensure that each ball in the covering set does not cross the boundary.

In practice, the
\delta
parametrization assumes that, before parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT), the pre-trained model ensures the initial parameters are already close to a specific local minimum. For example, in Fig.
5
, the parameters are assumed to be located in
\mathcal{H}_{1}\cup\mathcal{H}_{2}\cup\mathcal{H}_{3}
. This is a natural and mild assumption because PEFT starts from a pre-trained model and performs only small updates (e.g., via adapters or low-rank adjustments). The margin
\delta
is chosen as
O(1/N)
, which is very small. Hence, the assumption is very easy to achieve. Moreover, we don’t need all of the local solutions; we only need to choose a subset. So, by properly choosing these local solutions, we can easily meet the assumption.

Appendix M
Additional Experiment Settings

M-A

Construction of Training meta-distribution
\mathcal{E}

Consider the training class distribution represented by
P_{1},P_{2},...,P_{C}
, where
C
denotes the number of classes. Without loss of generality,
we assume that the classes are sorted in a sense
:
P_{1}\geq P_{2}\geq...\geq P_{C}
. Otherwise, we can change the numbering and obtain the same result.

The training meta-distribution
\mathcal{E}
is a Dirichlet mixture distribution as Sec.
VII-D
. It consists of three Dirichlet distribution components: the forward component
\alpha^{(f)}
, the uniform component
\alpha^{(u)}
, and the backward component
\alpha^{(b)}
.

The forward component parameter
\alpha^{(f)}
is set element-wisely:

\displaystyle\alpha^{(f)}_{i}={S\cdot P_{i}},\ i=1,2,\ldots,C

where
S
is a predefined normalization factor to control the variance of the component.
Here we set it as
S=10000
for all datasets.
Since the mean of the Dirichlet distribution is exactly
\alpha^{(f)}
, this component represents local variations concentrated around the long-tail distribution aligned with the training data.

The backward component
\alpha^{(b)}
is set element-wisely as:

\displaystyle\alpha^{(b)}_{i}={S\cdot P_{(C-i)}}

where
S
is a predefined normalization factor to control the variance of the component.
Here we set it as
S=10000
for all datasets.
By connecting
\alpha^{(b)}_{i}
with
P_{(C-i)}
, the long-tail distribution is reversed, where the head classes become tail classes, and
vice versa
. Since the mean of the Dirichlet distribution is exactly
\alpha^{(b)}
, this component represents local variations concentrated around the
inverse
long-tail distribution aligned with the training data.

The uniform component
\alpha^{(u)}
is set element-wisely as:

\displaystyle\alpha^{(u)}_{i}=\frac{S}{C},\ i=1,2,\ldots,C

where
S
is a predefined normalization factor to control the variance of the component.
Here we set it as
S=10000
for all datasets.
By connecting
\alpha^{(b)}_{i}
with
1/C
, it recovers a uniform distribution. Since the mean of the Dirichlet distribution is exactly
\alpha^{(u)}
, this component represents local variations concentrated around the uniform distribution.

The corresponding p.d.f are expressed as:
\mathsf{Dir}^{(f)},\mathsf{Dir}^{(u)},\mathsf{Dir}^{(b)}
. Moreover, the three components are mixed with a uniform distribution. Above all, the p.d.f for the mixture distribution becomes:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\frac{1}{3}\cdot\mathsf{Dir}^{(f)}+\frac{1}{3}\cdot\mathsf{Dir}^{(u)}+\frac{1}{3}\cdot\mathsf{Dir}^{(b)}

M-B

Sampling Procedure of
\mathbb{P}_{te}
for the Monte Carlo Approximation

Initially, we employ a Monte Carlo method to sample a set of
\mathbb{P}_{te}
from the training meta-distribution
\mathcal{E}
and obtain the generated data

\mathcal{P}=\{\mathbb{P}_{j},\xi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{M}
for Monte Carlo approximation.

We implement the sampling process by repeating the following two-step procedure for
M
times.

1.

Randomly sampling a Dirichlet distribution component
\alpha
from
\{\alpha^{(f)},\alpha^{(u)},\alpha^{(b)}\}
with equal probability.

2.

Sampling a distribution
\mathbb{P}_{te}
from the Dirichlet distribution component
\alpha
.

M-C

Mini-Batch Construction

Denote the number of mini-batches as
B
. We sample
60\cdot B
label distributions to construct the dataset
\mathcal{P}
. We randomly sample 60 label distributions for each mini-batch without replacement in each epoch to get an unbiased estimation.

M-D

Construction of Testing Datasets

We employ two settings, SADE’s Setting and our setting, to construct our test data. The details are discussed as follows:

M-D
1
SADE’s setting

The test data in this setting directly follows SADE
[
27
]
. The only difference here is that we include more imbalance ratios, denoted as
\rho
, in our experiments. For CIFAR 100-LT and CIFAR 10-LT,
\rho\in\{2,5,10,25,50,100\}
. For ImageNet-LT,
\rho\in\{2,5,10,25,50\}
. For iNaturalist,
\rho\in\{2,3\}
.

M-D
2
Ours setting

According to our meta-distribution, we employ three kinds of Dirichlet components to generate test distributions: the forward Dirichlet distribution
\alpha^{(f_{test})}
, the uniform Dirichlet distribution
\alpha^{(u_{test})}
and the backward Dirichlet distribution
\alpha^{(b_{test})}
.

From each component of the meta-distribution, we sample three specific distributions as the test class distribution, resulting in a total of
9 test class distributions
.

The Dirichlet distributions are chosen based on a predefined imbalance ratio
\rho
. The greater the
\rho
, the more challenging the test distribution.
For CIFAR 100-LT and CIFAR 10-LT, we set
\rho
to be 100. For ImageNet-LT, we set
\rho=50
. For iNaturalist, we set
\rho=3
.

For the forward Dirichlet distribution
\alpha^{(f_{test})}
, we set:

\displaystyle\alpha^{(f_{test})}_{i}=\rho^{-(i-1)/(C-1)},\ i=1,2,\ldots,C

For the backward Dirichlet distribution
\alpha^{(b_{test})}
, we set:

\displaystyle\alpha^{(b_{test})}_{i}=\rho^{-(C-i)/(C-1)},\ i=1,2,\ldots,C

For the uniform Dirichlet distribution
\alpha^{(u_{test})}
, we set:

\displaystyle\alpha^{(u_{test})}_{i}=1/C,\ i=1,2,\ldots,C

Subsequent steps remain consistent across the three components. For simplicity, we denote each Dirichlet distribution as
\alpha
.

To enhance the simulation of the randomness in the test distribution, we introduce a perturbation to
\alpha
, allowing up to 5% variations. This involves adjusting the
i
-th element
\alpha_{i}
:

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i}*\epsilon,\ \epsilon~\sim~U(-0.05,0.05)

Following this, we normalize
\alpha
to ensure the sum of its components equals
S
. The role of
S
is identical to its function in constructing the Training meta-distribution. We set
S=100
for CIFAR 100-LT,
S=1000
for CIFAR 10-LT,
S=10000
for ImageNet-LT and
S=100000
for iNaturalist.

N-E

Experts Assignment

In this part, we validate the ability of the test-time self-supervised aggregation by visualizing the weight assignments for different label distributions in Fig.
11
-
12
. The forward and backward experts always tend to have a significant weight for their corresponding distributions. Uniform distributions tend to utilize all three experts. This is because tail and head classes are equally crucial for uniform distribution.

(a)

Our’s Setting

(b)

SADE’s Setting

Fig. 11:

Weight Assignment in the Self-supervised Aggregations on CIFAR-10.

F,U,B
represent the forward, uniform and backward distributions. For the x-axis,
F-1,F-2,F-3
in
(a)
denote the three observed label distributions in the test data respectively.
F-2,F-5,
\cdots
,F-100
represents the corresponding imbalance ratio of the forward distribution under SADE’s setting. The case for
the suffices of U and B
are similar.
Expert-F,U,B
represents the experts assigned to the forward, backward, uniform Dirichlet distribution, respectively.

(a)

Our’s Setting

(b)

SADE’s Setting

Fig. 12:

Weight Assignment in the Self-supervised Aggregations on ImageNet.

F,U,B
represent the forward, uniform and backward distributions. For the x-axis,
F-1,F-2,F-3
in
(a)
denote the three observed label distributions in the test data respectively.
F-2,F-5,
\cdots
,F-100
represents the corresponding imbalance ratio of the forward distribution under SADE’s setting. The case for
the suffices of U and B
are similar.
Expert-F,U,B
represents the experts assigned to the forward, backward, uniform Dirichlet distribution, respectively.

N-F

Fine-grained Performance

In addition to the overall accuracy of test datasets, we also examine the effectiveness of DirMixE across many-shot, medium-shot, and few-shot classes. For CIFAR 100-LT and ImageNet-LT, classes are divided into many-shot (
>100
), medium-shot (
20\sim 100
), and few-shot (
<20
) categories. In the case of CIFAR 10-LT, classes are split into many-shot (
>1000
), medium-shot (
200\sim 1000
), and few-shot (
<200
) categories. All these statistics are based on the training label distribution. The results are illustrated in heat maps in Fig.
13
for CIFAR-10.

(a)

Our’s Setting

(b)

SADE’s Setting

Fig. 13:

Fine-grained Performance of DirMixE on CIFAR-10-LT

F,U,B
represent the forward, uniform and backward distributions. For the x-axis,
F-1,F-2,F-3
in
(a)
denote the three observed label distributions in the test data respectively.
F2,F5,
\cdots
,F100
in
(b)
represents the corresponding imbalance ratio of the forward distribution under SADE’s setting. The case for
the suffixes of U and B
are similar.
L, M, S
denote the many-shot, medium-shot and few-shot classes respectively. For example,
L-B100
indicates the performance of medium-shot classes on the backward distribution with an imbalance ratio of 100.

N-J

Ablation Study on the Effect of the Normalization Factor
S

As shown in Tab.
XXXVIII
-Tab.
XLI
, our investigation into
S
, the sum of the Dirichlet distribution hyperparameter
\alpha
, shows performance improves with higher
S
values before a slight decline. A larger
S
reduces the randomness in the sampled test-label distributions. When
S
is too small, local variation overshadows global trends, leading to misassigned experts and performance loss. Conversely, excessively large
S
values minimize local variation, making the meta-distribution collapse to fix distributions. Thus, we find a moderate value is optimal.

on CIFAR-100-LT (SADE’s Setting)

N-K

The Effect of The Number of Experts

(a)

Our’s Setting

(b)

SADE’s Setting

Fig. 14:

The Effect of Using Different Number of Experts.

In the
\mathsf{DirMixE}
method, three experts are initially considered. However, the
\mathsf{DirMixE}
framework can be extended to accommodate varying numbers of experts. We explore the impact of the number of experts on CIFAR 100-LT, specifically examining cases where the number is set to 1, 2, 3, 5, 7, and 9, where each expert corresponds to a distinct Dirichlet distribution component.

The forward and backward components are characterized by an imbalance ratio denoted as
\rho
.

Denote the training class distribution as
P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{C}
, where
C
denotes the number of classes.

Specifically, we will need the following notations for experts handling different levels of imbalance ratio.

The forward component
\alpha^{(f_{\rho})}
with imbalance ratio
\rho
is set element-wisely as:

\displaystyle\alpha^{(f_{\rho})}_{i}=\frac{S*\rho^{(i-1)/(C-1)}}{\sum_{j=1}^{C}\rho^{(j-1)/(C-1)}},\ i=1,2,\ldots,C,

where
S
is the normalization factor.

The backward component
\alpha^{(b_{\rho})}
with imbalance ratio
\rho
is set element-wisely as:

\displaystyle\alpha^{(b_{\rho})}_{i}=\frac{S*\rho^{(C-i)/(C-1)}}{\sum_{j=1}^{C}\rho^{(j-1)/(C-1)}},\ i=1,2,\ldots,C

where
S
is the normalization factor.

The uniform component
\alpha^{(u)}
is set element-wisely as:

\displaystyle\alpha^{(u)}_{i}=\frac{S}{C},i=1,2,\ldots,C

where
S
is the normalization factor.

In the case of a single expert, we utilize one Dirichlet component
\alpha^{(u)}
. For two experts, we employ two components,
\alpha^{(f_{100})}
and
\alpha^{(b_{100})}
. For three experts, we select the uniform component
\alpha^{(u)}
and the forward and backward component with
\rho=100
. For scenarios with more than three experts, we will symmetrically add the forward components and backward components based on the 3-experts configuration. For five experts, we select the uniform component
\alpha^{(u)}
and the forward/backward components with
\rho=100,50
. For seven experts, we select the uniform component
\alpha^{(u)}
and the forward/backward component with
\rho=100,50,25
. For nine experts, we select the uniform component
\alpha^{(u)}
and the forward/backward components with
\rho=100,50,25,10
.

The results are reported in Fig.
14
. This indicates that the average ACC is monotonically increasing as more and more experts are employed.
